See also: [A beginner’s guide to modern classical music](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/how-to-fall-in-love-with-modern-classical-music-4/). Also, [remember to get good headphones!](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/the-critical-importance-of-good-headphones/)

![June, 1977](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/wp-content/uploads/Percy-Jimmy-Heath.jpg)

“Art jazz,” as I use the term, refers to that subset of jazz music that, rather than faithfully savoring the old styles, or re-interpreting old standards, tries to express new musical ideas, _a la_ most [modern classical music](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/how-to-fall-in-love-with-modern-classical-music-4/). Unfortunately, there are few guides to modern art jazz that are _aimed at the beginner_. The best guide I know of is by Piero Scaruffi, who rightly [complains](http://www.scaruffi.com/history/jazz.html):

> Most books on the history of jazz music, even the ones published very recently… tend to devote 80–90% of the pages to jazz before the Sixties, and then to quickly summarize (with countless omissions) the last 40 years… The paradox, of course, is that a lot more has happened “since” the 1960s than “until” the 1960s… by far, the greatest contributions of jazz to the history of humankind came in the second half of the century, for example with composers (repeat: composers) such as Charlie Mingus, Ornette Coleman and John Coltrane.
>
> …It is hard to name \[Louis\] Armstrong in the same sentence with Mozart or Stravinsky, but not difficult at all to mention Ornette Coleman or Anthony Braxton with those heavy-weights of classical music.

[Scaruffi’s history of jazz](http://www.scaruffi.com/history/jazz.html) thus focuses on the development of art jazz since ~1960. My contribution here is to focus on an even smaller set of pieces: art jazz since 1960 that is _plausibly accessible to many new listeners_. (So, no [Anthony Braxton](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthony_Braxton) or [Borbetomagus](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borbetomagus).)

I should also mention that while most jazz fans think mostly about jazz _performers_, I think mostly about jazz _composers_. Thus, many famous albums don’t appear in this guide because they are mostly filled with covers. Instead I focus on novel jazz compositions that push the art of jazz composition forward in some way. My focus on composers also means that many albums credited to a _performer_ (e.g. Paul Bley’s _Closer_) are instead credited below to their primary _composer_ (Carla Bley, in the case of _Closer_).[1](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#footnote_1_1336 "There are also many albums I simply haven’t been able to cheaply and efficiently access for listening, e.g.: Richard Abrams’ Variations for Solo Saxophone Flute and Chamber Orchestra, Quintet for Soprano Piano Harp Cello and Violin, Improvisation Structures, Odyssey of King, Saturation Blue, String Quartet #2, String Quartet #3, Saxophone Quartet #1, NOVI; George Lewis’ Virtual Discourse, Ring Shout Ramble; Joe McPhee’s In Black and White; Egberto Gismonti’s Fábula de la Bella Palomera; Gerry Hemingway’s Terrains; Jane Ira Bloom’s We Are; Misha Mengelberg’s Saxophone Concerto; Willem Breuker’s Muziek In Amsterdam; Gunter Hampel’s Trailblazer, Generator, Life On This Planet, Vogelfrei, All Is Real, Celebrations, Angel, I Love Being With You, Unity Dance; Louis Andriessen’s Reconstructie; Peter Brotzmann’s Free Jazz Und Kinder; Franz Koglmann’s Join!; Werner Dafeldecker’s Ant Ort, Bonsai Beat, Insections; Graham Collier’s Memories Arrested in Space; Art Lande’s Times and Places; Wolfgang Dauner’s Dauner-eschingen; Jack Walrath’s Revenge of the Fat People, A Plea for Sanity; Daniel Kobialka’s Softness of a Moment; Michael Jones’ Windsong; Trilok Gurtu’s Farakala; Eugene Chadbourne’s Horror Part One; Samm Bennett’s Metafunctional, Ready Question; Jim Staley’s OTB; Toshinori Kondo’s Panta Rhei; Joe Maneri’s Kalavinka; Tyondai Braxton’s The Grow Gauge; Markus Stockhausen’s KölnMusik Fantasy; Fred Ho’s Red Arc; Vienna Art Orchestra’s Serapionsmusic. This list was updated in June 2025.")

_Note_: This guide links to lots of music on YouTube or Spotify: wherever the music is available. While reading, I recommend you Ctrl+click (Cmd+click on Mac) to open those links in a new browser tab so you can hear the music play for a bit before reading further. Some of the Spotify links point to custom playlists I made, since many of the original albums that aren’t (directly) available on Spotify _are_ reconstructable entirely from later compilation albums that _are_ on Spotify. When I couldn’t find an album on Spotify or YouTube, I linked to another source instead. [Here](https://open.spotify.com/user/lukeprog/playlist/74WvVYluGenq6iNoivekMR) is a Spotify playlist of all the bolded albums listed below (in the main text, not those in the footnotes), in chronological order. Remember, these are not the “best” albums of art jazz. Instead, they are some of the best albums of art jazz that (1) are plausibly accessible to many new listeners, and that (2) are available on Spotify.

**Update 1/7/2018**: Albums I’ve listened to more recently, from all style categories, are in **[this Google doc](https://docs.google.com/document/d/1WD_4isN9uK1Xw63PD5sp5Bc-pv_xAU5M4dt9uMhXmac/edit)**, since I don’t have time to add them here.

## A quick review of jazz up to ~1960

First, some context.

Jazz music emerged in the American South as a blend of ragtime, blues, spirituals, brass band music, and other influences. Depending on what you want to count as “jazz,” its origins date to somewhere from 1890–1915. The first jazz _recordings_ came a bit later. A sampling of early jazz:

- Jelly Roll Morton, “ [Jerry Roll Blues](https://open.spotify.com/track/1YkOdt6NjbIHIHhwrRBywW)” (published 1915, recorded later)[2](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#footnote_2_1336 "I typically only mention differences between writing/recording/publishing dates when there is more than a 2-year gap. When I list a year for recording, writing, or publishing, it is the last year during which that happened for the piece in question.")
- Original Dixieland Jass Band, “ [Dixie Jass Band One-Step](https://open.spotify.com/track/1hdJTXS3lcn2DR0QoT4zbt)” (1917)
- Louis Armstrong, “ [Heebie Jeebies](https://open.spotify.com/track/44WgRCq0K8ZUAm70QMhFZV)” (1926)
- Duke Ellington, “ [East St Louis Toodle-Oo](https://open.spotify.com/track/1zpy8qhdWMtE9d2Zy4v4kA)” (1926)
- Bix Beiderbecke, “ [In a Mist](https://open.spotify.com/track/5Bk13n28rlG7GYCKDshsQM)” (1927)
- Various, “ [Whiteman Stomp](https://open.spotify.com/track/1atY0jQgXZA2ldBcpc4kJ7)” (1927)

Many different threads of jazz music grew from this base.[3](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#footnote_3_1336 "If you’d like to understand jazz, I recommend Gridley’s Jazz Styles. There isn’t much difference between the 10th and 11th editions, and you can get a used copy of the 10th edition very cheaply. The book guides you not only through the major developments and styles of jazz, but also includes detailed “listening guides” for many famous jazz pieces, so that you can read what the major musical events are (at particular time markings) as you listen to the piece.")

The highly danceable **swing jazz** — think Benny Goodman, “ [Sing, Sing, Sing](https://open.spotify.com/track/5L8ta4ECl5zeA6bGqY7G38)” — dominated American popular music from roughly 1935–1945.

[Raymond Scott](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raymond_Scott) wrote frantic, quirky jazz pieces that Carl Stalling later licensed for use in cartoon soundtracks, for example “ [Powerhouse](https://open.spotify.com/track/1LmGTM3Cem6sBRmaX8EdVQ)” (1937).

**Bebop** (aka _bop_) dropped the emphasis on danceability in favor of more complex structures, an often faster-than-danceable pace, and an existential mood, e.g. in Gillespie’s “ [A Night in Tunisia](https://open.spotify.com/track/3WJo6BjKmIcEqaRsXCQf6f)” (1942), Monk’s “ [Round about Midnight](https://open.spotify.com/track/0HuzNvBg7tIQ91kLXO6f5p)” (1944), Parker’s “ [Ko ko](https://open.spotify.com/track/4FSDfXVrRDWyVYnSKVYEGw)” (1945), and Tristano’s “ [Spontaneous Combustion](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=gIjfOyUizx4)” (1947).

**Cool jazz** focused even less than bebop on rhythm and melody, and had a more relaxed tone, e.g. in MacGregor’s “ [Moon Dreams](https://open.spotify.com/track/4D6wQ5bAsFcYJGhZVxSxWV)” (1948), Getz’s “ [Early Autumn](https://open.spotify.com/track/1I7lenzwUlWkqqPbvW82P6)” (1948), Mulligan’s “ [Venus de Milo](https://open.spotify.com/track/1NUT97i0da9a1eWRkgen8U)” (1949), Konitz’s “ [Subconscious-Lee](https://open.spotify.com/track/4sjtlPTqNNE381vW5HPQ9A)” (1949), and Modern Jazz Quartet’s “ [Vendôme](https://open.spotify.com/track/0RboqLS2QMpRspvZ5GPScZ)” (1952).

**Hard Bop** was an outgrowth of bebop with simpler melodies and a more energetic sound, e.g. in Brown’s “ [Daahound](https://open.spotify.com/track/2s1H8IG0ajYEduKsHip7RB)” (1954), Silver’s “ [The Preacher](https://open.spotify.com/track/0ggCtQTH4GxoynMT9Lu7nN)” (1955), Rollins’ “ [Valse Hot](https://open.spotify.com/track/3v0NFJuoke1YPjwqe0j3nA)” (1956), and Mobley’s “ [Lower Stratosphere](https://open.spotify.com/track/2Ragvw98LXLYwxfnOg6olG)” (1957).

**Free jazz** discarded fixed chord progressions and tempos, e.g. in Tristano’s “ [Intuition](https://open.spotify.com/track/1oVjXnECqlEG9EhyuAmiGV)” (1949) and “ [Descent into the Maelstrom](https://open.spotify.com/track/5czn4Oi4mUpSr7yg30X5mV)” (1953), Giuffre’s “ [Fugue](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fhWecucsZio)” (1953), Taylor’s “ [Toll](https://open.spotify.com/track/593SNoBbAIlriWllvdzwpk)” (1958), and Coleman’s “ [Peace](https://open.spotify.com/track/4TrzjMsOnd1KWkWrsDRB3u)” (1959).

## Classic albums

In search of relatively _accessible_ art jazz from ~1960 onward, let’s start with the most-played art jazz tracks that were released from 1959-1970.[4](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#footnote_4_1336 "To construct this list, I began with the recently-most-played jazz tracks according to last.fm, removed tracks released outside the 1959-1970 window (e.g. tracks by Norah Jones), removed tracks that weren’t really part of the stylistic vanguard at the time (e.g. Rollins’ version of “God Bless the Child” from The Bridge), and collapsed tracks from the same album into a single line item.") Obviously this isn’t a pure measure of accessibility, since people will often play tracks based on what they’re _told_ is good rather than what they find enjoyable. But here’s the list (as of this writing) nonetheless:

01. Davis, _[Kind of Blue](https://open.spotify.com/album/1weenld61qoidwYuZ1GESA)_ (1959, hard bop)[5](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#footnote_5_1336 "Kind of Blue and many other albums on this page are often classified as primarily “modal jazz.” I won’t treat modal jazz as a distinct genre in this guide, since it’s more of a compositional technique than a “style.” So in practice and for the most part, there is modal jazz that sounds like bebop, modal jazz that sounds like cool jazz, modal jazz that sounds like hard bop, etc.")
02. Desmond’s “ [Take Five](https://open.spotify.com/track/1hVXZoSAkZ3P5Lp6fKOcgx)” and Brubeck’s “ [Blue Rondo à la Turk](https://open.spotify.com/track/7d43jBR2mo0kcKcrO2Hg4S)” & “ [Strange Meadow Lark](https://open.spotify.com/track/6Y9FGnmx9PXsmgLs02UirG)” & “ [Kathy’s Waltz](https://open.spotify.com/track/6qNWmjlMAW503WLZLfjUba)” from _Time Out_ (1959, cool jazz)[6](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#footnote_6_1336 "Note that when I refer to an individual track, I always name the composer, even if the track is usually attributed to the lead performer rather than the composer. But when I refer to albums, I refer to whomever the album is usually credited to, even if that’s not the lead composer. I think this is unfair to the composer, but it’s one of many shortcuts I decided to take in order to finish this guide before the heat death of the universe.")
03. Mingus’ “ [Goodbye Pork Pie Hat](https://open.spotify.com/track/5eKnpzuUKdgjAKqEpWtbwD)” & “ [Fables of Faubus](https://open.spotify.com/track/7DoBL69h4MBOiq5bRvMBzp)” & “ [Boogie Stop Shuffle](https://open.spotify.com/track/11cHTwgw5L3qrNcv5Ugv0O)” & “ [Better Git it in Your Soul](https://open.spotify.com/track/16nJl8NnriCJxraco5Zssm)” & “ [Self-Portrait in Three Colors](https://open.spotify.com/track/38C8v33eis3fa4hLf0dl8Z)” from _Mingus Ah Um_ (post-bop)
04. Coltrane’s “ [Naima](https://open.spotify.com/track/360beQMahQ2FdZ9OmWYiBT),” “ [Giant Steps](https://open.spotify.com/track/18KSLe1ScEmgFt1XnyRSCV),” “ [Cousin Mary](https://open.spotify.com/track/1WHR4UpQNFysgxJV5uaxMi),” and “ [Countdown](https://open.spotify.com/track/0m9kjCcQ6jSGBNuTAr0d0i)” from _Giant Steps_ (1960, hard bop)
05. Coltrane’s “ [My Favorite Things](https://open.spotify.com/track/3ZikLQCnH3SIswlGENBcKe)” from _My Favorite Things_ (1961, post-bop)
06. Hancock’s “ [Cantaloupe Island](https://open.spotify.com/track/0sCeNwt8xRCMR4NhKpMyBe)” from _Empyrean Isles_ (1964, post-bop)
07. Hancock’s “ [Watermelon Man](https://open.spotify.com/track/1VfGDylBa6VHNi1pcxRx1Z)” from _Takin’ Off_ (1962, hard bop)
08. Timmons’ “ [Moanin’](https://open.spotify.com/track/4Tq2fWpX1nLCkMSOPkYb1Y)” from _Moanin’_ (1959, hard bop)
09. Hancock’s “ [Maiden Voyage](https://open.spotify.com/track/0T1EaGm6b9eM7xBf4F1hlc)” from _Maiden Voyage_ (1965, post-bop)
10. Davis’ “ [Pharaoh’s Dance](https://open.spotify.com/track/7eEp1mm5xRfPMpilLFUKwX)” from _Bitches Brew_ (1970, jazz-rock)

If you find yourself liking the bebop, cool jazz, and hard bop selections above, there’s a good chance you’ll like nearly _all_ of the well-reviewed albums from those genres, and you can just listen to all the best albums from those genres on lists compiled elsewhere, including:[7](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#footnote_7_1336 "There are also a few albums from this period with a relatively accessible “classic” style — e.g. swing — that, unlike most albums in their style, were trying to push artistic boundaries. Judging which albums meet this criteria is of course pretty subjective, but one likely uncontroversial example is Duke Ellington’s Far East Suite (1967). (Jazz historians sometimes distinguish “classic” jazz from “modern” jazz. The latter began with bebop and includes everything that came after.)")

- [RYM’s chart](http://rateyourmusic.com/customchart?page=1&chart_type=top&type=album&year=alltime&genre_include=1&include_child_genres=1&genres=bebop%2C+hard+bop%2C+cool+jazz&include_child_genres_chk=1&include=both&origin_countries=&limit=none&countries=) of top-ranked albums in bebop, cool jazz, and hard bop. (The most accessible albums will typically be those where the _first_ genre listed is bebop, cool jazz, or hard bop. The exception is when “modal jazz” — more of a compositional technique than a “style” — is listed as the first genre: in those cases, treat the 2nd-listed genre as the primary genre.)
- An [Allmusic advanced search](http://www.allmusic.com/advanced-search) for “main albums” rated 4.5 or 5 stars in bop, bop vocals, cool, or hard bop. (In this case, it will be harder to quickly filter for albums that are _primarily_ bebop, cool jazz, or hard bop. One way to improve the average accessibility of the items in the search results list is to further restrict the advanced search to albums released from 1945–1962. But this will still return some experimental albums like _[L’Ascenseur Pour l’Échafaud](https://open.spotify.com/album/4pocTDVRT3269NRVpSduXe)_.)
- The albums from Piero Scaruffi’s [Best Jazz Albums](http://www.scaruffi.com/jazz/best100.html) list that are primarily bebop, cool jazz, or hard bop: _[Kind of Blue](https://open.spotify.com/album/1weenld61qoidwYuZ1GESA)_, _[Crosscurrents](http://www.allmusic.com/album/crosscurrents-mw0000919302)_, _[Brilliant Corners](https://open.spotify.com/album/497oRf9Gj92hSsPq5izn8A)_, _[Saxophone Colossus](https://open.spotify.com/album/2dtjLAwt7Cq763h6AupyPZ)_, _[Fontessa](https://open.spotify.com/album/4ceTTa0JKnKsppT43oe8dc)_, _[Giant Steps](https://open.spotify.com/album/7cZ6oBx0SEUPDAoxJtxNDh)_, _[Go!](https://open.spotify.com/album/5nEJj9bjoarnzlS88NiWet)_, _[Birth of the Cool](https://open.spotify.com/album/3wG8DcAJVgr7sbWNvKanzS)_, and _[Freedom Suite](https://open.spotify.com/user/lukeprog/playlist/74scvXrb6JqMusC812CbHu)_.[8](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#footnote_8_1336 "I checked Scaruffi’s list for bebop, cool jazz, and hard bop albums up through selection #100, as of 06-05-2015.")

## Post-bop

From here on, I’ll be listing a _lot_ of albums, so I’ll also begin to **bold** the standout albums. Since my own methods for judging the best in jazz are still developing, I decided to simply bold all albums appearing on Scaruffi’s ranked decade lists, which he says are more accurate than his [overall list](http://scaruffi.com/jazz/best100.html). Since Scaruffi has no decade lists starting with the 2000s, I’ve bolded Scaruffi’s #1 album for each year after 1999.[9](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#footnote_9_1336 "I should also mention that, part-way through developing this guide, I stopped providing release dates and hyperlinks for albums included in the footnotes but not in the main text, so that’s why that inconsistency exists. It was simply too much tedious work.")

The way I’m using the term, “post-bop” is a catch-all term for jazz music that is heavily influenced by bebop and/or hard bop, but which either “moves beyond” bebop/hard bop in various stylistic ways (e.g. the Davis quintet’s albums below), or which borrows heavily from other styles (world musics, free jazz, etc.), without being _primarily_ free jazz, avant-garde jazz, jazz-rock, or some other major jazz genre.[10](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#footnote_10_1336 "Post-bop is a contested term. Some sources consider hard bop a subgenre of post-bop. Others don’t necessarily do that, but they do have a much broader definition of post-bop than I do, and thus trace its origins all the way back to, say, 1945.")

Because post-bop is so varied, some varieties of it are a lot more accessible than other varieties, so I can’t as easily say “If you like these 5 examples of post-bop, you’ll probably like all the best-reviewed albums in the genre,” as I did with bebop, hard bop, and cool jazz. So, this is where I start providing some real value, since I’m not aware of anyone else who has tried to make a long list of the most _accessible_ good albums of post-bop.

According to one critic,[11](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#footnote_11_1336 "Namely, Jeremy Yudkin. He includes Miles in the Sky and Filles de Kilimanjaro as post-bop, but I’m counting them as jazz-rock here.") the founding albums of post-bop — which are all pretty accessible, to my ears — were recorded from 1965–1968 by Miles Davis’ 2nd quintet:

- _[E.S.P.](https://open.spotify.com/album/16l5pMsJoz1KoHrxvXREBs)_ (1965), _[Miles Smiles](https://open.spotify.com/album/7buEUXT132AA4FPswvh9tV)_ (1967), _[Sorcerer](https://open.spotify.com/album/55tHsdolIq77ph1iiepCJt)_ (1967), _[Nefertiti](https://open.spotify.com/album/4ilAxtGR8aOj6M8X1HeHPY)_ (1968)

Personally, I would trace the origins of post-bop much further back, at least to Charles Mingus. To my ears, the most accessible of his many great albums are:[12](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#footnote_12_1336 "Presents Charles Mingus isn’t listed here due largely to the relatively challenging “What Love?” In some cases I included an album if it only contains one short “inaccessible” track, for example I included Oh Yeah despite “Passions of a Man.” Mingus’ later works are excluded here for containing some relatively inaccessible sections, but if you like Mingus you might want to check them out anyway: Mingus Moves (1973), Changes One (1974), Changes Two (1975), and Cumbia & Jazz Fusion (1978).")

- **_[Pithecanthropus Erectus](https://open.spotify.com/album/4kZ55sjjRKgCphjOmSMR7o)_** (1956), _[The Clown](https://open.spotify.com/album/2XtydLjClSxUt6uMA9Zhpb)_ (1957), **_[Tijuana Moods](https://open.spotify.com/album/4kaQvqgh3GVhDhV0OeNYIw)_** (rec. 1957, rel. 1962),[13](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#footnote_13_1336 "This album will also appear in my “world jazz” section, later in the post.") [_Jazz Portraits: Mingus in Wonderland_](https://open.spotify.com/album/4dkPMU6yIGsMW7oyhexxg0) (1959), [_Blues & Roots_](https://open.spotify.com/album/7EOQggjtK8JCqeRz9IG33e) (1959), **_[Mingus Ah Um](https://open.spotify.com/album/4Z8nWz3dGybOP7lhMZpcOo)_** (1959), _[Mingus Dynasty](https://open.spotify.com/user/lukeprog/playlist/2Z42VTh6a47DpKXKCBgNMm)_ (1959), _[Mingus](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mingus_(Charles_Mingus_album))_ (1961), **_[Oh Yeah](https://open.spotify.com/album/4tj38XID2vHZmlYxOVR9v4)_** (1962), **_[The Black Saint and the Sinner Lady](https://open.spotify.com/user/lukeprog/playlist/1DdkYJW1gkd1tFhRHQbzE3)_** (1963), _[At UCLA](http://www.allmusic.com/album/at-ucla-mw0000770486)_ (rec. 1965, rel. 2006)

Here are some other good and (relatively) accessible post-bop albums, grouped by artist:[14](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#footnote_14_1336 "Excluded for inaccessibility: Charles Lloyd’s Dream Weaver (1966), Forest Flower (1968), Hagar’s Song; Steve Lacy’s Momentum (1987), Let’s Call This… Esteem! (1993), Vespers (1993); Karl Ikonen’s Beauteous Tales and Offbeat Stories; Empirical’s Empirical. Excluded for dullness: Takuya Kuroda’s Edge, Rising Son; Bennie Maupin’s Penumbra; Gregory Porter’s Water; Kurt Elling’s The Gate; Eric Reed’s The Baddest Monk; Chris Dingman’s Waking Dreams; Manu Katche’s Neighborhood, Playground, Third Round; Ingrid Laubrock’s Who Is It?, Some Times; Wolfgang Muthspiel’s Solo, The Promise; Tigran Hamasyan’s A Fable; Melissa Aldana’s Back Home; Jack DeJohnette’s The DeJohnette Complex; Kenny Barron’s The Art of Conversation, Sunset to Dawn, Peruvian Blue, Things Unseen, Four for All, Images; Wolfgang Dauner’s Dream Talk, Music Zounds; Jessica William’s And Then There’s This, Inventions; Joachim Kühn’s Nightline New York; Joe Lovano’s Tones Shapes & Colors, Solid Steps, Village Rhythm, Friendly Fire, Universal Language, Trio Fascination, Flights of Fancy, Worlds; Lyle Mays’ Fictionary; Bobby Watson’s Appointment in Milano; Tom Harrell’s Moon Alley, The Art of Rhythm, Time’s Mirror; Terence Blanchard’s Wandering Moon, Terence Blanchard, Simply Stated, Romantic Defiance; Mulgrew Miller’s Hand in Hand; Fred Hersch’s Horizons; Joshua Redman’s Freedom in the Groove, Beyond, Passage of Time; Brad Mehldau’s Ode; Don Rendell’s Space Walk; Kenny Clarke’s Jazz Convention Volume II; Jack Duff’s The Enchanted Isle; Joe Haider’s Cafe Des Pyrennees; Uptown String Quartet’s Just Wait a Minute!")

- Sun Ra: _[Jazz by Sun Ra](https://open.spotify.com/album/6sCBzSHjDYeyMAnBX29pj1)_ (1956), _[Super-Sonic Jazz](https://open.spotify.com/album/350IC9cg0xI7RTr78yYcRZ)_ (1956), **_[Jazz in Silhouette](https://open.spotify.com/album/3isFbNpscHoeo7xHXkrM7A)_** (1959), _[Visits Planet Earth](https://open.spotify.com/album/1Dk7VBUVEnWUcvpgednNyQ)_ (rec. 1958, rel. 1966), _[The Futuristic Sounds of Sun Ra](https://open.spotify.com/album/7JWnG9HqckM8R5Lqx5jPV3)_ (1961)[15](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#footnote_15_1336 "Ra’s other albums aren’t primarily post-bop.")

I excluded Left & Right (1968) because its central composition, the 20-minute “Expansions,” is probably too inaccessible for this list. I did include Rahsaan Rahsaan, though, because only a few minutes of “The Seeker” are particularly difficult. I excluded Natural Black Inventions: Root Strata (1971), Prepare Thyself To Deal With a Miracle (1973), The Return of the 5000 Lb. Man (1976) because they’re not mostly post-bop, and several of their tracks are not particularly accessible. Blacknuss (1972) is an unusually creative album of covers, but it’s an album of covers nonetheless, so I excluded it from this list.")

## World jazz

I use the term “world jazz” for albums which fuse jazz styles (e.g. post-bop) with very heavy doses of world folk musics. Some recommended and accessible albums are:[97](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#footnote_97_1336 "Excluded for not being jazzy enough: Choying Drolma’s Cho, Selwa; L. Shankar’s Evening Concert, Raga Aberi, Eternal Light, Celestial Body, Who’s to Know, Song for Everyone, Pancha Nadai Pallavi, Soul Searcher; Steve Gorn’s Yantra, Luminous Ragas, From the Caves of the Iron Mountain. Excluded for dullness: Rail Band’s Orchestre Rail Band de Bamako; Bembeya Jazz National’s 10 ans de succes; Barney Wilen’s Moshi. Excluded for inaccessibility: Big Black’s Message to Our Ancestors; The Pyramids’ King of Kings, Lalibela; Mor Thiam’s Dini Safarrar; Maulawi’s Maulawi.")

## Third stream\*

Gunther Schuller coined the term “third stream” in 1957 to refer “a new genre of music located about halfway between jazz and classical music.” (Had Schuller known that Chuck Berry was inventing rock music that very moment, perhaps he would have coined the term “fourth stream” instead.)

Schuller clarified that “third stream” music wasn’t jazz played with classical instruments, nor classical music played by a jazz band, nor the insertion of a bit of Ravel between bebop chord changes, nor a jazz fugue. Rather, third stream music is compositionally inspired by classical and jazz music roughly equally, although Schuller stressed that improvisation must be important in third stream music as it is in jazz.[118](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#footnote_118_1336 "A few of the pieces I listed in the “Blurred Lines” section of my beginner’s guide to contemporary classical music probably count as third stream, but I’m going to cover the genre more thoroughly here in my jazz guide.")

Milhaud’s _[La Création du monde](https://open.spotify.com/user/lukeprog/playlist/18iuHkaoW9fp6X8EyD1eYv)_ (1923) and Gershwin’s _[Rhapsody in Blue](https://open.spotify.com/track/7oB0QBEnGRIJjvlDc8YnM6)_ (1924) and [other early fusion attempts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_stream#Earlier_fusion_attempts) mixed classical and jazz influences, though most of them didn’t count as third stream music by Schuller’s definition because they didn’t involve improvisation.

On this page, I’m going to be less strict than Schuller was about the improvisation rule, and I’ll “compensate” for that by preferring composers who came primarily from the jazz world rather than primarily from the classical world. Call it “third stream\*” if you like.

Then, from among third stream\* recordings, I’ll try to list the ones which seem most “accessible”:[119](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#footnote_119_1336 "I considered including Mingus’ Jazzical Moods (1955), but I don’t think is classical enough to be called third stream*. I made the same call for Davis’ Sketches of Spain (1960). Examples of third stream* music that weren’t accessible enough for this list include: Lateef’s African-American Epic Suite (rec. 1993, rel. 1996), Weber’s The Colours of Chloë (1974), Coleman’s Skies of America (1972), Giuffre’s Piece For Clarinet And String Orchestra (1960), Alice Coltrane’s Lord of Lords (1972) and World Galaxy (1972), Macero’s What’s New? (1956), Mann’s Concerto Grosso in D Blues (1968), Penderecki’s Actions for Free Jazz Orchestra (1971), Horn’s Jazz Suite on the Mass Texts (1965), and Burton’s Seven Songs for Quartet and Chamber Orchestra (1973).")

- Stan Kenton: “ [Trajectories](https://open.spotify.com/track/5yevLOqYsRn1fx7qPphVSg)” (1950), “ [Theme for Sunday](https://open.spotify.com/track/7fzHCKhu0qfPZqKWMQAbgR)” (1950)[120](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#footnote_120_1336 "Stan Kenton’s early albums — Stan Kenton Presents (1950), Innovations in Modern Music (1950), City of Glass (1952), New Concepts in Artistry of Rhythm (1952), This Modern World (1953) — are definitely third stream*, but they generally aren’t accessible enough for this list, which is why I selected particular tracks from them instead. Kenton’s New Concepts of Artistry in Rhythm (1952) and several later albums are accessible enough, but not mostly third stream*. Kenton returned to third stream* music later, e.g. on Conducts the Los Angeles Neophonic Orchestra (1965), but I decided these albums weren’t reliably accessible enough for this list.")
- Modern Jazz Quartet: _[Django](https://open.spotify.com/album/5dARhcAgd8MavsMPVweOXJ)_ (1955), “ **[Fontessa](https://open.spotify.com/track/6Idvhn4u9xtUTl9IipiiiR)**” (1956), _[Third Stream Music](https://open.spotify.com/album/09EtDVuuAy1qkxuC6gcmTs)_ (1960), _[The Comedy](https://open.spotify.com/album/493PU0ujjGi9zflCLcgrjG)_ (1962)[121](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#footnote_121_1336 "Most of Concorde (1955) and Fontessa (1956) isn’t third stream*, so I included only the most third stream* track from those albums, “Fontessa.”")
- Charles Mingus: “ [Half-Mast Inhibition](https://open.spotify.com/track/1xKcLOpf8tteqXBB0f7HOf)” (1960), **_[Epitaph](https://open.spotify.com/album/5CD7txbtMSasADYZuWqloD)_** (written by 1962, rel. 1990), **_[Let My Children Hear Music](https://open.spotify.com/album/03zH4bkGb2jSZAQIVW2px1)_** (1972)
- Jim Hall: “ [Piece for Guitar & Strings](https://open.spotify.com/track/5rmMIKGHoulP2WxKroQ6tj)” (1960)
- Gunther Schuller: “ [Variants for Jazz Quartet and Orchestra: Variant II](https://open.spotify.com/track/0sRP3D5VcV0rKBAyHamGgn)” (1960)[122](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#footnote_122_1336 "This was the most accessible-sounding piece by Schuller I could find. He wrote a lot of third stream* music, but almost none of it is accessible enough for this list.")
- Dizzy Gillespie: _[Perceptions](https://open.spotify.com/album/3lqRzHr6xnt6b4WpA4Jncr)_ (1961)
- Stan Getz: _[Focus](https://open.spotify.com/album/6XcAnjX7vbsDVfbj8PYTmo)_ (1961)
- Moondog: _[Moondog](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=aK0yv9ME-h8)_ (1969), _[Sax Pax for a Sax](https://open.spotify.com/album/4AWNKGLUOuN8Wd4H0JJhRb)_ (rec. 1994, rel. 1997)[123](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#footnote_123_1336 "My judgment is that Moondog’s earlier albums are not very accessible, and rarely third stream*.")
- Bill Evans: _[Symbiosis](http://www.allmusic.com/album/symbiosis-mw0000177041)_ (1974)
- Turtle Island String Quartet: _**[Turtle Island String Quartet](https://open.spotify.com/album/2xjQyjAIjKBaBoGFAPr0jv)**_ (1988), _[Metropolis](https://open.spotify.com/album/4KvbWXnI5MpzQGd3CNjCtA)_ (1989), _[Skylife](https://open.spotify.com/album/0971Ocbsd4H73kYqUgjbNG)_ (1990), _[Spider Dreams](https://open.spotify.com/album/3fiFblUCBGwTXhMWY3Tsxq)_ (1992)

(For more albums, see [my supplemental Google doc](https://docs.google.com/document/d/1WD_4isN9uK1Xw63PD5sp5Bc-pv_xAU5M4dt9uMhXmac/edit).)

For a while, it looked like third stream music might be the future of jazz. Instead, free jazz came along and crushed it. Then, after the free jazz revolution, the basic idea of third stream was reborn in “creative” jazz, this time heavily influenced by free jazz.

## Free jazz

“Free jazz” is what you’d guess from the name. It’s jazz that often does one or more of the following: (1) forgoes planned chord progressions, (2) forgoes planned tempos, (3) forgoes planned melodies in favor of pure improvisation, and/or (4) forgoes other standard compositional or performance assumptions. At the extreme, it consists in a bunch of players improvising for 10+ minutes without any pre-planned rhythms or chord progressions or modes or _anything_. As such, much of free jazz sounds like random screeching noises to most people, and it requires serious effort to find true examples of free jazz that are at least _somewhat_ “accessible.” As such, my standards for what counts as “accessible” are even looser for this section than they are for most other sections.

Though the earliest free jazz tracks go back to [1944](http://mattendahl.blogspot.com/2013/07/free-improvisation-series-robert-crum.html), the genre is usually considered to have been launched in the late 1950s, by Cecil Taylor and Ornette Coleman. As it happens, Coleman also produced a few of the most melodic and accessible albums of free jazz:

- **_[The Shape of Jazz to Come](https://open.spotify.com/album/2iPH3iUmpa9ufIpwY76keF)_** (1959), _[Change of the Century](https://open.spotify.com/album/0ME2xPIm65f5cJve3ALqdi)_ (1960), _[Ornette on Tenor](https://open.spotify.com/album/2a23A4d5QnNHJKE2raAh6H)_ (1962)[124](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#footnote_124_1336 "I decided Free Jazz (1960), This Is Our Music (1961), Ornette! (1961), Chappaqua Suite (1965), Who’s Crazy (1966), Ornette at 12 (1968), and Science Fiction (1972) weren’t accessible enough for this section. Skies of America (1972) is third stream*. New York is Now! (1968) and Love Call (1968) are accessible enough, but not very “free.” Most of Coleman’s later work is jazz-rock or jazz-funk.")

Other good and _relatively_ accessible free jazz albums and tracks include:[125](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#footnote_125_1336 "Many, many good albums of free jazz were excluded from this list due to insufficient accessibility, including: John Coltrane’s Ascension (1965), Sun Ship (rec. 1965, rel. 1971), Om (1965), and others; Albert Ayler’s Spirits (rec. 1964, rel. 1966), New York Eye and Ear Control (1965), Spiritual Unity (1965), Ghosts (1965), Bells (1965), Spirits Rejoice (1965), Love Cry (1968), Music is the Healing Force of the Universe (1969); Sun Ra’s Heliocentric Worlds Vol 1 (1965), Vol 2 (1966), & Vol 3 (rec. 1965, rel. 2005), The Magic City (1966), Atlantis (1969), When Angels Speak of Love, Featuring Pharoah Sanders And Black Harold; Sam Rivers’ Dimensions & Extensions (rec. 1967, rel. 1986), Streams (1973), Crystals (1974), The Quest (1976), Paragon (1977), Waves (1978), and Tangens (1997); Marion Brown’s Marion Brown Quartet (1966), Juba-Lee (1966), Three for Shepp (1966), Porto Novo (1968), Duets, Why Not?; Archie Shepp’s Fire Music (1965), On This Night (1966), Mama Too Tight (1966), The Way Ahead (1969), and Blasé (1969); Alice Coltrane’s Universal Consciousness (1971); Dave Burrell’s Echo (1969), After Love (1970), High Won-High Two, La Vie de Boheme, Windward Passages [1979]; Bill Dixon’s Intents and Purposes (1967), Opium (rec. 1969, rel. 2001), Vade Mecum (1994), Vade Mecum II (1996), The Enchanted Messenger (1994), Considerations 1 (rec. 1976, rel. 1981), Considerations 2 (rec. 1976, rel. 1980), Collection (rec. 1976, rel. 1999), and Berlin Abbozzi (2000); ## Creative jazz

“Creative” jazz, in my usage, refers more to a _school_ than to a _style_, but it has its own section here because there _are_ stylistic similarities between the artists in the creative school, and because otherwise I’d have to dump all these artists into the already-too-large-and-diverse “other avant-garde jazz” section.

In 1965, Muhal Richard Abrams co-founded the Association for the Advancement of Creative Musicians ( [AACM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Association_for_the_Advancement_of_Creative_Musicians)), a non-profit devoted to “nurturing, performing, and recording serious, original \[jazz\] music.” The style of AACM composers, or thoroughly AACM-influenced composers, was most often a blend of free jazz and the classical avantgarde, with a special focus on the timbres and textures of the music. In a stylistic sense, creative jazz was a continuation of third stream music. But culturally, it was more directly a descendent of the free jazz tradition.

![Braxton 76 excerpt](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/wp-content/uploads/Braxton-76-excerpt.jpg)Creative jazz is home to many of the most complicated, intellectually challenging compositions of jazz, comparable to e.g. the works of [Babbitt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milton_Babbitt), [Stockhausen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karlheinz_Stockhausen), or [Ferneyhough](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brian_Ferneyhough) from the classical tradition. Some creative jazz composers, especially [Anthony Braxton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthony_Braxton), wrote long, dense volumes on music theory, invented special musical notations to capture their musical ideas, and published intimidating-looking musical scores like the one to the right (from the [score](http://tricentricfoundation.org/all-scores/composition-no-76) for Braxton’s _Composition No. 76_.)

I should mention that if an artist is clearly part of the “creative” tradition, I’m not going to think very hard about whether an album is best characterized as post-bop, avantgarde jazz, jazz fusion, creative jazz, or indeed contemporary classical. If it sounds like any of those and the composer is firmly in the creative jazz tradition, I’m usually just going to classify it as creative jazz. For this reason, most of the albums listed below _are not very representative_ of “creative jazz,” because the albums accessible enough to make this list tended to be “on the border” with other genres, but that happened to be composed by a musician associated with the creative school.

As with my free jazz section, my standards for what counts as “accessible” in this section must be even looser than they are for most other sections. Also as with my free jazz section, most of the key albums in this genre will only be mentioned in footnotes, because they aren’t as accessible as those below:[136](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#footnote_136_1336 "Excluded for inaccessibility: Anthony Braxton’s Three Compositions of New Jazz, For Alto, Anthony Braxton, Silence, The Complete Braxton 1971, Together Alone, Saxophone Improvisations Series F, Creative Music Orchestra ‎RBN—-3° K12, Town Hall 1972, Live at Moers Festival, Four Compositions, First Duo Concert, Royal Volume 1, Trio and Duet, New York Fall 1974, Five Pieces, Creative Orchestra Music 1976, Time Zones, Elements of Surprise, Duets (w/ Abrams), Donaueschingen, Dortmund, Duets (w/ Mitchell), For Trio, Quintet (Basel) 1977, Solo (Koln) 1978, Creative Orchestra 1978, For Four Orchestras, Alto Saxophone Imrovisations, Birth and Rebirth, Performance (Quartet) 1979, Composition No. 94, Open Aspects, For Two Pianos, One in Two — Two in One, Composition No. 96, Four Pieces, Composition 98, Composition 113, Seven Compositions 1978, Ensemble (Victoriaville), Four Compositions (Quartet), The Aggregate, 19 Solo Compositions, Five Compositions (Quartet), Six Compositions (Quartet), Eugene, 4 Ensemble Compositions, Composition 173, Two Lines, Trillium R — Shala Fears for the Poor, Composition 174, Ensemble [New York] 1995, Octet [New York] 1995, Tentet [Antwerp] 2000, Composition 102, Anthony Braxton with the Creative Jazz Orchestra, Composition 165, 11 Compositions, Composition 192, 10 Compositions (w/ Fonda), Ninetet (Yoshi’s) Vols. 1-4, Tentet (New York) 1995, Four Compositions (Quartet) 1995, Beyond Quantum, Four Compositions (Washington D.C.) 1998, Composition No. 169 + (186 + 206 + 214), Creative Orchestra (Bolzano 2007), Trillium E, Trio (Victoriaville) 2007, 9 Compositions (Iridium) 2006; ## Jazz fusion

Creative jazz was always a difficult, obscure tradition with a limited audience. After free jazz, the next “mainstream” movement within jazz was jazz _fusion_. Starting in the late 1960s, dozens of artists fused jazz with rock, R&B, funk, country, and even pop music. Many of the jazz greats who had gone through post-bop and free jazz phases next turned their attention to fusion.

As jazz artists stepped toward rock, rock artists stepped toward jazz: see Frank Zappa, Soft Machine, Colosseum, Caravan, and Chicago. Below, I focus on fusion artists who primarily trace their lineage to jazz rather than rock (or country, etc.), even though some fusion artists from the rock tradition recorded albums that were “jazzier” than some of the fusion albums recorded by artists from the jazz tradition. (Note that I’ve excluded all “new age jazz” that I felt was closer to “new age” than to “jazz.”)[183](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#footnote_183_1336 "For example, I’ve excluded the following albums as “too new age”: Mark Isham’s Vapor Drawings, Tibet, Castalia, The Beast, Songs My Children Taught Me; Michael Jones’ Amber, Pianoscapes, Seascapes, Sunscapes, After the Rain, Magical Child, Morning in Medonte, Air Born; David Darling’s Journal October, Cycles, The Tao of Cello, Cello, 8-String Religion, Cello Blue, Prayer for Compassion, Dark Wood; George Winston’s Autumn, December, The Velveteen Rabbit; Georgia Kelly’s Seapeace, Ancient Echoes, Birds of Paradise, A Journey Home, Gardens of the Sun, Tarashanti; Daniel Kobialka’s Timeless Motion, Fragrances of a Dream, When You Wish Upon a Star, Velvet Dreams, Path of Joy, Somewhere Over the Rainbow; David Lanz’s Heartsounds, Nightfall, Christofori’s Dream, Skyline Firedance; Liz Story’s Solid Colors, Escape of the Circus Ponies; Georgia Kelly’s The Sound of Spirit; Teja Bell’s Summer Suite; Montreux’s Sign Language; Steve Kindler’s Dolphin Smiles; Michael Shrieve’s The Big Picture, Stiletto, In Suspect Terrain, The Leaving Time; Paul Winter’s Common Ground, Callings, Missa Gaia, Sun Singer, Canyon, Whales Alive, Prayer for the Wild Things, Callings, Canyon Lullaby, Celtic Solstice, Journey with the Sun, Miho Journey ot the Mountain, Crestone, Something in the Wind, Concert for the Earth.")

Unsurprisingly, jazz fusion is often the genre of jazz most accessible to those raised on rock and pop music, so this section contains a _lot_ of recommended “accessible” albums. It is also probably the jazz genre with the highest number of popular but artistically dubious/worthless albums. The result is that whereas the vast majority of “notable” albums in free jazz and creative jazz were relegated to my footnotes as “inaccessible” (even given my relaxed accessibility standards for those genres), the vast majority of “notable” albums in _fusion_ jazz are relegated to my footnotes as “dull” — i.e., not artistically ambitious, or just _bad_.

As usual, I suggest you start with the **bolded** albums.[184](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#footnote_184_1336 "Excluded for dullness: Flora Purim’s albums; Chico Freeman’s The Mystical Dreamer, Sweet Explosion, Thresshold; Leni Stern’s Clairvoyant, Secrets, Africa; Snarky Puppy’s Sylva, The Only Constant; George Benson’s The New Boss of Guitar, It’s Uptown, Giblet Gravy, The Shape of Things to Come, Beyond the Blue Horizon, Body Talk, Breezin’, Give Me the Night; Spyro Gyra’s Spyro Gyra, Catching the Sun, Incognito, Breakout, Road Scholars; Al Jarreau’s We Got By, This Time, Jarreau; Yellowjackets’ Yellowjackets, Mirage a Trois, Greenhouse, Run for Your Life, Blue Hats, Mint Jam; Kenny G’s Duotones, Paul McCandless’ Navigator; Lyle Mays’ Sweet Dreams, Lyle Mays; Branford Marsalis’ Buckshot Lefonque, Music Evolution; Stanley Jordan’s Magic Touch, Touch Sensitive; Cassandra Wilson’s New Moon Daughter, Belly of the Sun, Glamoured; Kurt Rosenwinkel’s The Next Step; Mark Isham’s Group 87; Andy Narell’s The Hammer, Little Secrets; Shadowfax’s Shadowdance; Akira Ishikawa’s Bakishinba; Alex Rodriguez’s Busqueda; Bingo Miki’s Mystic Solar Dance; Michael Shrieve’s Fascination, Two Doors; Jimmy Giuffre’s Dragonfly. Excluded for barely being jazz: Gil Scott-Heron’s Small Talk at 125th and Lenox, Pieces of a Man, Spirits, Winter in America, First Minute of a New Day, From South Africa to South Carolina, Bridges, Secrets, 1980, I’m New Here; Samm Bennett’s History of the Last Five Minutes, Life of Crime, The Big Off. Excluded for inaccessibility: Mark Nauseef’s Sura, The Snake Music, Wun-Wun; Nels Cline’s Silencer, Ground, Edible Flowers, Instrumentals, The Giant Pin, Chest, Sad, Draw Breath, Coward, Initiate, Dirty Baby, Tocsin; Semantics’ Semantics; Elliott Sharp’s Datacide.")

## ECM style jazz

[ECM style jazz](https://rateyourmusic.com/genre/ECM+Style+Jazz/),[258](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#footnote_258_1336 "As always, different critics and historians put different boundaries around their genre terms. Some people would count e.g. Paul Bley’s (really, Carla Bley’s) Closer (1965) as ECM style jazz. For my own convenience, I’m going to hue pretty close to RYM’s definition of ECM style jazz.") named after the record label that released many of the earliest albums in this style, combines several elements:

- free jazz’s willingness to abandon structure for lengthy periods of total improvisation
- cool jazz’s relatively subdued aesthetic (not free jazz’s violent, dissonant aesthetic)
- third stream’s interest in classical forms (not so much creative jazz’s interest in less accessible post-1940 contemporary classical music)

Describing the earliest ECM style artists, RYM [adds](https://rateyourmusic.com/genre/ECM+Style+Jazz/):

> Their approach is usually described as “ascetic,” “restrained,” or “meditative” and their playing can be characterized by long, slow-pacing gestures that are preferred to displays of virtuosity, usage of silence, subdued expressivity and attention to “spatial” organization in music. They approach their instruments in more traditional way compared to free jazz, not pushing them to their expressive limits. ECM style jazz is tonal, although it doesn’t operate with instantly recognizable melodies, it is often quite static and close to Impressionism in its treatment of textures and atmospheres. Rhythmically the music is straight (often in straight eight-notes) and doesn’t have the “swing” feel that’s common to majority of jazz. The adjectives usually associated with ECM style are “dreamy,” “ethereal,” “icy,” etc.

Here are some relatively accessible ECM style jazz albums:[259](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#footnote_259_1336 "Excluded for inaccessibility: Paul Bley’s Open to Love, In the Evenings Out There.")

## Other avant-garde jazz

This is my catch-all category for avant-garde jazz that I couldn’t really place under other headings, such as free jazz or creative jazz. Most albums in this category are too inaccessible for this list,[267](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#footnote_267_1336 "Bill Dixon’s In Italy Vol 1, In Italy Vol 2, November 1981, Thoughts, Son of Sisyphus, Papyrus Vol 1, Papyrus Vol 2, 17 Musicians in Search of a Sound: Darfur, Tapestries for Small Orchestra; Marion Brown’s Afternoon of a Georgia Faun, Geechee Recollections, Sweet Earth Flying; John Carter’s Dauwhe, Castles of Ghana, Dance of the Love Ghosts, Shadows on a Wall, Fields; Steve Lacy’s The Cry, The Window, Clangs; Roswell Rudd’s Flexible Flyer; John Tchicai’s Cadentia Nova Danica, Afrodisiaca, Put Up the Fight, Love is Touching, Anybody Home?; Miles Davis’ Aura; Charlie Haden’s Golden Number; Michael Mantler’s Communication, Jazz Composer’s Orchestra; Keith Rowe’s A Dimension of Perfectly Ordinary Reality, Harsh, Dial Log-rhythm, Queue, The Hands of Caravaggio, Wigry, Electric Chair + Table, Rabbit Run, Duos for Doris, The Room, Afternoon Tea, Weather Sky, Cloud; Jack Wright’s The Darkest Corner the Most Conspicuous; Joe Zawinul’s Mauthausen; Hans Reichel’s Shanghaied on Tor Road; Larry Karush’s May 24, 1976; Glen Moore’s Introducing; Keith Tippett’s Warm Spirits Cool Spirits, Frames; Basil Kirchin’s Quantum, Particles, Words Within Worlds [1971], Worlds Within Worlds [1974]; Wolfgang Dauner’s Fur, Psalmus Spei; Jaap Blonk’s Consensus, Speechlos; Andrew Cyrille’s The Declaration of Musical Independence; Cecilia Persson’s Composer in Residence; Thumbscrew’s Convallaria; Tania Chen’s Ocean of Storms; Kris Davis’ The Slightest Shift, Rye Eclipse, Good Citizen, Massive Threads, Capricorn Climber, Waiting for You to Grow, Save Your Breath, Duopoly; Spring Heel Jack’s Masses, Amassed, The Sweetness of Water, Songs and Themes; Bob James’ Explosions; Grachan Moncur’s Some Other Stuff; Jacques Berrocal’s Paralleles, Musiq Musik; Richard Landry’s 4 Cuts Placed in A First Quarter; Kafka’s Ibiki’s Kafka’s Ibiki; New Direction’s Call in Question; Tony Scott’s Voyage into a Black Hole; Michael Wollny’s Wunderkammer.") even given more relaxed standards for what counts as “accessible” (as I did for the free jazz and creative jazz categories). But here are some (highly diverse) exceptions:[268](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#footnote_268_1336 "Excluded for not being close enough to “jazz”: Lyle Mays’ Twelve Days In The Shadow Of A Miracle; Jack DeJohnette’s Music in the Key of Om; Zeena Parkins’ Spill; Eyvind Kang’s Virginal Co-ordinates, The Story of Iceland, Live Low to the Earth in the Iron Age; Supersilent’s 13; Mats Gustafsson’s Apertura; Richard Landry’s Fifteen Saxophones. Excluded for dullness: Ian Carr’s Sounds and Sweet Airs; Terence Blanchard’s The Malcolm X Jazz Suite; Matt Ulery’s By a Little Light; Craig Taborn’s Chants; Bobo Stenson’s Cantando; Tomasz Stanko’s Tales for a Girl; Andy Milne’s Scenarios.")

001. There are also many albums I simply haven’t been able to cheaply and efficiently access for listening, e.g.: Richard Abrams’ _Variations for Solo Saxophone Flute and Chamber Orchestra_, _Quintet for Soprano Piano Harp Cello and Violin_, _Improvisation Structures_, _Odyssey of King_, _Saturation Blue_, _String Quartet #2_, _String Quartet #3_, _Saxophone Quartet #1_, _NOVI_; George Lewis’ _Virtual Discourse_, _Ring Shout Ramble_; Joe McPhee’s _In Black and White_; Egberto Gismonti’s _Fábula de la Bella Palomera_; Gerry Hemingway’s _Terrains_; Jane Ira Bloom’s _We Are_; Misha Mengelberg’s _Saxophone Concerto_; Willem Breuker’s _Muziek In Amsterdam_; Gunter Hampel’s _Trailblazer_, _Generator_, _Life On This Planet_, _Vogelfrei_, _All Is Real_, _Celebrations_, _Angel_, _I Love Being With You_, _Unity Dance_; Louis Andriessen’s _Reconstructie_; Peter Brotzmann’s _Free Jazz Und Kinder_; Franz Koglmann’s _Join!_; Werner Dafeldecker’s _Ant Ort_, _Bonsai Beat_, _Insections_; Graham Collier’s _Memories Arrested in Space_; Art Lande’s _Times and Places_; Wolfgang Dauner’s _Dauner-eschingen_; Jack Walrath’s _Revenge of the Fat People_, _A Plea for Sanity_; Daniel Kobialka’s _Softness of a Moment_; Michael Jones’ _Windsong_; Trilok Gurtu’s _Farakala_; Eugene Chadbourne’s _Horror Part One_; Samm Bennett’s _Metafunctional_, _Ready Question_; Jim Staley’s _OTB_; Toshinori Kondo’s _Panta Rhei_; Joe Maneri’s _Kalavinka_; Tyondai Braxton’s _The Grow Gauge_; Markus Stockhausen’s _KölnMusik Fantasy_; Fred Ho’s _Red Arc_; Vienna Art Orchestra’s _Serapionsmusic_. This list was updated in June 2025. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_1_1336)\]
002. I typically only mention differences between writing/recording/publishing dates when there is more than a 2-year gap. When I list a year for recording, writing, or publishing, it is the _last_ year during which that happened for the piece in question. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_2_1336)\]
003. If you’d like to _understand_ jazz, I recommend Gridley’s [_Jazz Styles_](http://smile.amazon.com/Jazz-Styles-11th-Mark-Gridley/dp/020503683X/). There isn’t much difference between the 10th and 11th editions, and you can get a used copy of the 10th edition [very cheaply](http://smile.amazon.com/gp/offer-listing/0136005896/ref=dp_olp_used?ie=UTF8&condition=used). The book guides you not only through the major developments and styles of jazz, but also includes detailed “listening guides” for many famous jazz pieces, so that you can read what the major musical events are (at particular time markings) as you listen to the piece. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_3_1336)\]
004. To construct this list, I began with the recently-most-played jazz tracks [according to last.fm](http://www.last.fm/tag/jazz/tracks), removed tracks released outside the 1959-1970 window (e.g. tracks by Norah Jones), removed tracks that weren’t really part of the stylistic vanguard at the time (e.g. Rollins’ version of “God Bless the Child” from _The Bridge_), and collapsed tracks from the same album into a single line item. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_4_1336)\]
005. _Kind of Blue_ and many other albums on this page are often classified as primarily “modal jazz.” I won’t treat modal jazz as a distinct genre in this guide, since it’s more of a compositional technique than a “style.” So in practice and for the most part, there is modal jazz that sounds like bebop, modal jazz that sounds like cool jazz, modal jazz that sounds like hard bop, etc. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_5_1336)\]
006. Note that when I refer to an individual track, I always name the composer, even if the track is usually attributed to the lead performer rather than the composer. But when I refer to albums, I refer to whomever the album is usually credited to, even if that’s not the lead composer. I think this is unfair to the composer, but it’s one of many shortcuts I decided to take in order to finish this guide before the heat death of the universe. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_6_1336)\]
007. There are also a few albums from this period with a relatively accessible “classic” style — e.g. swing — that, unlike most albums in their style, _were_ trying to push artistic boundaries. Judging which albums meet this criteria is of course pretty subjective, but one likely uncontroversial example is Duke Ellington’s _[Far East Suite](https://open.spotify.com/album/1JQ7TkPUI1x04CCZLpYMzU)_ (1967). (Jazz historians sometimes distinguish “classic” jazz from “modern” jazz. The latter began with bebop and includes everything that came after.)  \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_7_1336)\]
008. I checked Scaruffi’s list for bebop, cool jazz, and hard bop albums up through selection #100, as of 06-05-2015. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_8_1336)\]
009. I should also mention that, part-way through developing this guide, I stopped providing release dates and hyperlinks for albums included in the footnotes but not in the main text, so that’s why that inconsistency exists. It was simply too much tedious work. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_9_1336)\]
010. Post-bop is a contested term. Some sources consider hard bop a subgenre of post-bop. Others don’t necessarily do that, but they do have a much broader definition of post-bop than I do, and thus trace its origins all the way back to, say, [1945](http://www.allmusic.com/album/zodiac-suite-mw0000646253). \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_10_1336)\]
011. Namely, [Jeremy Yudkin](http://smile.amazon.com/Miles-Davis-Smiles-Invention-Post/dp/0253219523/). He includes _Miles in the Sky_ and _Filles de Kilimanjaro_ as post-bop, but I’m counting them as jazz-rock here. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_11_1336)\]
012. _[Presents Charles Mingus](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Mingus_Presents_Charles_Mingus)_ isn’t listed here due largely to the relatively challenging “What Love?” In some cases I included an album if it only contains one short “inaccessible” track, for example I included _Oh Yeah_ despite “Passions of a Man.” Mingus’ later works are excluded here for containing some relatively inaccessible sections, but if you like Mingus you might want to check them out anyway: _[Mingus Moves](https://open.spotify.com/album/1XpvxdpyiuSTJTApwYjHgG)_ (1973), _[Changes One](https://open.spotify.com/album/254OYgcNQJIccVL79HIWxr)_ (1974), _[Changes Two](https://open.spotify.com/album/7o9hJ8konXzGt6nKLy3MuX)_ (1975), and _[Cumbia & Jazz Fusion](https://open.spotify.com/album/1A8nmVkCixB6meKXWN9Okj)_ (1978). \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_12_1336)\]
013. This album will also appear in my “world jazz” section, later in the post. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_13_1336)\]
014. Excluded for inaccessibility: Charles Lloyd’s _[Dream Weaver](https://open.spotify.com/album/3lAHM9enj2ctgKZ1hFnA5i)_ (1966), _[Forest Flower](https://open.spotify.com/album/6lIWfnUerZuu9UcUDnF2JD)_ (1968), _Hagar’s Song_; Steve Lacy’s _[Momentum](http://www.allmusic.com/album/momentum-mw0000197391)_ (1987), _[Let’s Call This… Esteem!](https://open.spotify.com/album/2zoszn2n3bfhHsMN9uzHNC)_ (1993), _[Vespers](http://www.allmusic.com/album/vespers-mw0000112946)_ (1993); Karl Ikonen’s _Beauteous Tales and Offbeat Stories_; Empirical’s _Empirical_. Excluded for dullness: Takuya Kuroda’s _Edge_, _Rising Son_; Bennie Maupin’s _Penumbra_; Gregory Porter’s _Water_; Kurt Elling’s _The Gate_; Eric Reed’s _The Baddest Monk_; Chris Dingman’s _Waking Dreams_; Manu Katche’s _Neighborhood_, _Playground_, _Third Round_; Ingrid Laubrock’s _Who Is It?_, _Some Times_; Wolfgang Muthspiel’s _Solo_, _The Promise_; Tigran Hamasyan’s _A Fable_; Melissa Aldana’s _Back Home_; Jack DeJohnette’s _The DeJohnette Complex_; Kenny Barron’s _The Art of Conversation_, _Sunset to Dawn_, _Peruvian Blue_, _Things Unseen_, _Four for All_, _Images_; Wolfgang Dauner’s _Dream Talk_, _Music Zounds_; Jessica William’s _And Then There’s This_, _Inventions_; Joachim Kühn’s _Nightline New York_; Joe Lovano’s _Tones Shapes & Colors_, _Solid Steps_, _Village Rhythm_, _Friendly Fire_, **_Universal Language_**, _Trio Fascination_, _Flights of Fancy_, _Worlds_; Lyle Mays’ _Fictionary_; Bobby Watson’s _Appointment in Milano_; Tom Harrell’s _Moon Alley_, _The Art of Rhythm_, _Time’s Mirror_; Terence Blanchard’s _Wandering Moon_, _Terence Blanchard_, _Simply Stated_, _Romantic Defiance_; Mulgrew Miller’s _Hand in Hand_; Fred Hersch’s _Horizons_; Joshua Redman’s _Freedom in the Groove_, _Beyond_, _Passage of Time_; Brad Mehldau’s _Ode_; Don Rendell’s _Space Walk_; Kenny Clarke’s _Jazz Convention Volume II_; Jack Duff’s _The Enchanted Isle_; Joe Haider’s _Cafe Des Pyrennees_; Uptown String Quartet’s _Just Wait a Minute!_ \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_14_1336)\]
015. Ra’s other albums aren’t primarily post-bop. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_15_1336)\]
016. Taylor’s later albums are primarily free jazz. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_16_1336)\]
017. Probably his only album that counts as post-bop. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_17_1336)\]
018. I consider these albums to be mostly post-bop with some elements of free jazz, whereas some of Coleman’s later work I classify as free jazz. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_18_1336)\]
019. I’m counting Waldron’s earlier albums, and also _Evidence_, as not _primarily_ post-bop. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_19_1336)\]
020. A few of these are arguably more free jazz than post-bop, but in the end I decided they were more post-bop than free jazz. I’m counting Coltrane’s later albums as not primarily post-bop. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_20_1336)\]
021. Excluded for dullness: _Pictures in a Frame_. Excluded for inaccessibility: _Scott Free_, _Live in Tokyo_. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_21_1336)\]
022. It was a tough call, but I decided _[Outward Bound](https://open.spotify.com/album/2vDH2vjdjyJtVGiDCHvUFP)_ (1960) wasn’t quite post-bop enough to make the main list. I couldn’t find a copy of _[Naima](http://www.allmusic.com/album/naima-mw0000273623)_ (1964) to evaluate, though Dolphy’s track from that album, “ [Springtime](https://open.spotify.com/track/4nD5KzmUWUwGpONBzXnveb),” is available on Spotify. I decided _[Conversations](https://open.spotify.com/user/lukeprog/playlist/3GUS8ny1BtAik5HN45Tps3)_ (1963) and _[Iron Man](https://open.spotify.com/album/6IJ5eucxSEPApZOZje8MmJ)_ (1963) weren’t accessible enough for this list. The famous _[Out to Lunch](https://open.spotify.com/album/6IJ5eucxSEPApZOZje8MmJ)_ (1964) is probably best classified as free jazz. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_22_1336)\]
023. Earlier albums are mostly hard-bop, and not world-y enough even to qualify as post-bop. Excluded for inaccessibility: _Psychicemtous_, _1984_. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_23_1336)\]
024. I decided that _[Blues for Smoke](https://open.spotify.com/album/1Tlg3Ey93Oo99eRD5LOCaO)_ (1960) wasn’t “mostly” post-bop, though it’s certainly accessible. I also decided that _[Freedom Together!](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_Together!)_ (1966) and _[Sunshine of My Soul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunshine_of_My_Soul)_ (1967) weren’t accessible enough for this list. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_24_1336)\]
025. _Liquid Dancers_ excluded because dull. _Thesis_ excluded because inaccessible. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_25_1336)\]
026. I decided _[Enlightenment](https://open.spotify.com/album/7MGSGfawOVDQqC3blhKDmc)_ (1973), [Sama Layuca](https://open.spotify.com/album/74JxnRC4sJf0brcrya2ibP) (1974), _[Atlantis](https://open.spotify.com/album/6JXzNrI1bYNieDG16JbCLF)_ (1974), _[4×4](https://open.spotify.com/album/58OIz2HCCWYt99kfreA3Fq)_ (1976), _[Supertrios](https://open.spotify.com/album/1TtB5dFMpBYlxaGPMV3Hqh)_ (1977), and others were too inaccessible for this list. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_26_1336)\]
027. Ellington’s other albums aren’t primarily post-bop. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_27_1336)\]
028. I’m counting McLean’s earlier albums as not _primarily_ post-bop. I also decided that McLean’s **_[New and Old Gospel](https://open.spotify.com/album/2w8CUvLWgSw0FdQ4vE2vR5)_** wasn’t accessible enough for the main list. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_28_1336)\]
029. I decided that _[Compulsion](https://open.spotify.com/album/4KL9IZQdZKRj3gtR90EVp0)_ (1965), _[Invitation](https://open.spotify.com/album/7HleRr02jNBh2DdezQzN1r)_ (1974) and the jazz opera _[Lift Every Voice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lift_Every_Voice_(Andrew_Hill_album))_ (1970) probably aren’t accessible enough for this list. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_29_1336)\]
030. I decided that _[Archie Shepp/Bill Dixon Quartet](http://www.allmusic.com/album/archie-shepp-bill-dixon-quartet-mw0000895755)_ (1962) and _[The New York Contemporary Five](http://www.allmusic.com/album/the-new-york-contemporary-five-mw0000241150)_ (1963) weren’t accessible enough for this list, and _Wo!Man_ was too dull. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_30_1336)\]
031. Rivers’ later albums are inaccessible and/or free jazz. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_31_1336)\]
032. I’m counting Shorter’s earlier albums as not primarily post-bop. Excluded for inaccessibility: _The All-Seeing Eye_, _Super Nova_. Excluded for dullness: _High Life_. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_32_1336)\]
033. I’m counting Kirk’s earlier albums as not primarily post-bop. I excluded _[Left & Right](https://open.spotify.com/album/53JxR61ZogrEhyQ27EerwQ)_ (1968) because its central composition, the 20-minute “Expansions,” is probably too inaccessible for this list. I did include _Rahsaan Rahsaan_, though, because only a few minutes of “The Seeker” are particularly difficult. I excluded **_[Natural Black Inventions: Root Strata](https://open.spotify.com/album/3ojyRBXDuMN1OJSKaFKuv9)_** (1971), **_[Prepare Thyself To Deal With a Miracle](https://open.spotify.com/album/7a4juPTEzCvCUjehORJjyj)_** (1973), _[The Return of the 5000 Lb. Man](https://open.spotify.com/album/2Yxoej9fLlVFH3IMc3kuDS)_ (1976) because they’re not mostly post-bop, and several of their tracks are not particularly accessible. _[Blacknuss](https://open.spotify.com/album/1OnXBqIDGKpI3Y9qugJ85p)_ (1972) is an unusually creative album of covers, but it’s an album of covers nonetheless, so I excluded it from this list. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_33_1336)\]
034. Excluded for inaccessibility: _Dialogue_, _Oblique_, _Components_. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_34_1336)\]
035. Probably his only album that might count as post-bop. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_35_1336)\]
036. Excluded for dullness: _Piano Improvisations Vol 1_, _Piano Improvisations Vol 2_, _Akoustic Band_. Excluded for inaccessibility: _Now He Sings Now He Sobs_, _The Song of Singing_. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_36_1336)\]
037. _Jazz Long Playing_ is just standards. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_37_1336)\]
038. I haven’t heard any of his other albums. His later albums are more fusion than post-bop. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_38_1336)\]
039. Excluded for dullness: _The Maker_. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_39_1336)\]
040. Excluded for dullness: _Angel Song_, _All the More_, _What Now?_, _Mirrors_, _Kayak_, _Dream Sequence_. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_40_1336)\]
041. Excluded for inaccessibility: _Life Between the Exit Signs_, _The Mourning of a Star_, _Birth_, _Expectations_, _Death and the Flower_, _Back Hand_, _Inside Out_. Meanwhile, _Restoration Ruin_ is folk rock, not jazz. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_41_1336)\]
042. I’m counting Henderson’s earlier albums as not _primarily_ post-bop. I also decided _Barcelona_ wasn’t accessible enough. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_42_1336)\]
043. _Blues and the Soulful Truth_ is pretty dull except for “Gypsy Queen.” \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_43_1336)\]
044. I decided _Brilliant Circles_ and both volumes of _Handscapes_ weren’t accessible enough for this list. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_44_1336)\]
045. I’m counting many of his later albums as primarily fusion, and most of his earlier albums as primarily hard bop. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_45_1336)\]
046. I’m counting some of Burton’s albums as ECM style jazz. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_46_1336)\]
047. I’m counting most of Sanders’ albums as not primarily post-bop. I’ve included these albums here despite a few not-so-accessible minutes of music here and there. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_47_1336)\]
048. Excluded for dullness: _The Opal Heart_, _Forgotten Fantasies_. Excluded for inaccessibility: _Open Sky_, _Of One Mind_, _The Loneliness of a Long Distance Runner_. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_48_1336)\]
049. Excluded for inaccessibility: Ecstasy. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_49_1336)\]
050. _The Windmills of Your Mind_ was excluded because it’s mostly covers, and relatively dull. Others were excluded for inaccessibility: _Conception Vessel_, _Le Voyage_, _The Story of Maryam_, _Jack of Clubs_, _It Should’ve Happened a Long Time Ago_. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_50_1336)\]
051. Excluded for dullness: _Invitation_, _Aft_, _Fi-Fi Goes to Heaven_, _Special Identity_. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_51_1336)\]
052. Excluded for dullness: _Characters_, _Up and Coming_. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_52_1336)\]
053. I’m counting Brown’s other albums as either insufficiently accessible, or some other genre (usually free jazz). \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_53_1336)\]
054. Some of Shaw’s other albums — in particular _[Blackstone Legacy](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blackstone_Legacy)_ (1970), _[Song of Songs](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Song_of_Songs_(Woody_Shaw_album))_ (1972), _[The Iron Men](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Iron_Men_(album))_ (rec. 1977, rel. 1980) — are also very good, but I decided they weren’t quite accessible enough to make the list for this article. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_54_1336)\]
055. Excluded for inaccessibility: _Meant to Be_, _Past Present_. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_55_1336)\]
056. Excluded for inaccessibility: _Just Us_, _Oh for the Edge_, _Boundaries_. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_56_1336)\]
057. Excluded for dullness or being too hard-bop: _Patrao_, _Parade_. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_57_1336)\]
058. _Bush Baby_ excluded for inaccessibility. _Put Sunshine On It_ and _Da-Da_ excluded for dullness. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_58_1336)\]
059. Excluded for dullness: _Night-Glo_. Excluded for inaccessibility: _Jazz Realities_. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_59_1336)\]
060. Excluded for dullness: _Black Codes_, _J Mood_, _The Majesty of the Blues_, _Blue Interlude_, _In This House On This Morning_, _Sweet Release and Ghost Story_, _Big Train_, _The Marciac Suite_, _From the Plantation to the Penitentiary_. Not post-bop: _Jump Start and Jazz_. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_60_1336)\]
061. Excluded for dullness: _Unforseen Blessings_, _Lord Riff and Me_, _Stablemates_, _Pied Piper_, _Oh By the Way_, _Salutes the Saxophone_, _Focus_. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_61_1336)\]
062. Excluded for dullness: _Manhattan Moonrise_, _Been Up So Long It Looks Like Down to Me_. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_62_1336)\]
063. Excluded for inaccessibility: _The Mystery of Compassion_. \[ [↩](https://lukemuehlhauser.com/a-beginners-guide-to-modern-art-jazz/#identifier_63_1336)\]
